CHAPTER 11
STATEMENTS
Introduction: A computer program is a finite set of instructions to be executed by the computer. In a programming language, these instructions are called statements. JavaScript instructions are executed by the interpreter of the browser. And JavaScript statements are made of values, constants, keywords, operators, operands etc. the statements are executed in the order in which they have been written. Each JavaScript statement must be terminated by a semicolon in order to write various statements in the same line.
Note: It is recommended to have a semicolon at the end of each statement but it is not mandatory. Set of instructions is sometimes called JavaScript code.
Let’s have a JavaScript code to add two numbers
<html>
<head><title>JavaScript Statements Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let a, b, c;
a = 4; b = 1; c = a + b;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = c;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Observe that more than one statement is written in a single line separated by a semicolon.
JavaScript code blocks: These are the finite set of instructions written within the curly braces {…}. These statements are called compound statements. The purpose of grouping instructions together in a block to perform some specific operation on it. We are familiar with an example to use curly braces in the JavaScript function.
JavaScript Keywords: keywords are the reserved words that have fixed meaning to the interpreter of the web browser that cannot be changed by the programmer. These keywords cannot be used as variable names.
Some of the keywords are listed in the table below.
keyword
Description
var
It is used to declare a variable.
let
It is used to declare a block variable.
const
It declares a block constant.
if
It is used to check the condition and returns Boolean value (either TRUE or FALSE value).
switch
It is used to choose a particular statement out of several alternatives available.
for
It is used to repeatedly execute a single statement or set of statements.
function
It is used to declare a function
return
It is used to exit from a function.
try
It is used in error handling.
JavaScript Syntax: Syntax is a grammar of any programming language. The programmer must follow the rules of the programming language to write the instructions in a specific programming language.
JavaScript values: The JavaScript syntax defines two types of values.
-          Fixed values
-          Variable values
Fixed values: The quantity that does not change during the execution of a program is called fixed value or Literals or Constants.
Example: any constant value with or without decimal like, 5, -10, 15.4 etc.
String constants written within double or single quotes like, “Hanumanth Ladwa” or ‘Hanumanth Ladwa’
Variable values: The quantity that changes during the execution of a program is called variables. These are the named memory locations to store values or constants. To declare the variables, JavaScript uses the keywords var, let and const. And the assignment operator is used to initialize the variables.
Example: let a;
a=10;
Operators: Arithmetic operators +, - , *, / are used to form an expression.
Example:
let a=2, b=3, c;
c=a+b;
c=a-b;
c=a*b;
c=a/b;
Expressions: expressions are the combination of operators and operands.
Example: operands may consist of only constants or combination of constants and variables or only variables.
Example:
let a,b,c;
c=3*5;
c=a+5;
c=a+b;
c=10+b;
var name=“Hanumanth” + “ “ + “Ladwa”; // strings will be concatenated
var x;
const y;
are a few examples.
Note: if the variables are declared without a value, then the variable will have the value undefined.
Comments: Comments are the textual information for the other programmer to understand the purpose of the program. These statements are ignored by the interpreter of the web browser. There are two types of comments in JavaScript.
-          Single line comment (//…)
-          Multiline comment (/*…*/)
Single line comment: This type of comment is written when there is a single line of text.
Example: // This is a single line comment
Multiline comment: This type of comment is written when there are multiple lines of text.
Example: /* This is a
multiline comment*/
Identifiers: Identifiers are the names given to the programming elements such as variables, labels, functions etc.
Rules for declaring identifiers in JavaScript:
-          The identifier name must begin with a letter (A to Z or a to z), or an underscore (_), or a dollar sign ($).
-          Both uppercase and lowercase letters are distinct. Example, sample is different from SAMPLE.
-          Keywords cannot be used as an identifier name.
-          Identifiers must have reasonable length.
-          It must not consist of special characters other than underscore and dollar symbol.
Examples: $name_of_the_company, _salary, fees etc.
Character set: JavaScript uses the Unicode characters that covers all the characters (A – Z or a – z), punctuations (@, #, !, ?, / “, ‘, etc) and digits (0 - 9).
Declaring variables using let: the variables defined with let cannot be redeclared, must be declared before use and they have block scope.
Example: let name=”Hanumanth”;
let name=”Priyanka”;​// This statement creates errors stating that name has // already been declared
but with var we can declare the same variable multiple times.
Example: var name=”Hanumanth”;
var name=”Priyanka”;
it has block scope, meaning that the variables declared inside a { and } creates a block and the variables declared with let cannot be accessed whereas, variables declared with var can be accessed outside the block as shown in the following example.
{
let a=10;
}
// a cannot be accessed here
Similarly when used the same variable with var.
{
var a=10;
}
// a can be accessed here
If the variable is redeclared as shown in the example below see what happens.
Example 1:
var a=10;
{
var a=5;
}
// here a is 5
Example 2:
let a=10;
{
let a=5;
}
// here a is 10
Declaring variables with const: variables declared with const cannot be redeclared, reassigned and they have block scope.
Example: const PI=22/7;
PI=3.142;​// This is not allowed, produces an error
In case of constant arrays,
const names = [“Priyanka”,”Sneha”,”Swaroop”];
names[0]=”Raj”;​// here you can change an element
names.push(“Rakshita”);​// you can add an element
but you cannot reassign the array,
const names = [“Priyanka”,”Sneha”,”Swaroop”];
names=[“Raj”,”Rakshita”];​// This produces an error
declaring a variable with const is similar to let when it comes to block scope.
Example, const a=10;
{
const a=20;
}
// here a is 10
Redeclaring a JavaScript var variable is allowed anywhere in a program
Example,
var a=2;
var a=3;
a=4;
redeclaring an existing var and let variable to const, in the same scope, is not allowed.
Example:
var a=2;​// Allowed
const a=3;​// Not allowed
{
let a=2;​// Allowed
const a=3;​// Not allowed
}
{
const a=2;​// Allowed
const a=3;​// Not allowed
}
Reassigning an existing const variable, in the same scope, is not allowed.
Example:
const a=2;​// Allowed
var a=3;​// Not allowed
let a=4; ​// Not allowed
const a=5; ​// Not allowed
{
const a=2;​// Allowed
var a=3;​// Not allowed
let a=4; ​// Not allowed
const a=5; ​// Not allowed
}
Redeclaring a variable with const, in another scope, or in another block, is allowed.
Example:
const a=10;​​// Allowed
{
const a=20;​​// Allowed
}
{
const a=30;​​// Allowed
}
Const hoisting: variables defined with var are hoisted to the top and can be initialized at any time. The variable can be used before it is declared.
Example:
Yourname=”Hanumanth”;
var Yourname;
variables defined with const are also hoisted to the top, but not initialized. Using a const variable before it is declared will result in a ReferenceError. Let’s have a look at programming examples to understand.
<html>
<head><title>JavaScript Hoisting</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
try {
alert(Yourname);
const Yourname = "Hanumanth";
}
catch (err) {
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = err;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:

CHAPTER 12
OPERATORS
Introduction: an operator is a symbol that tells the compiler what specific mathematical or logical manipulation has to be performed.
Arithmetic operators: The operators supported by JavaScript are: +, -, *, /, %, **, ++, --
Let us consider, a=10 and b=2 then
Operator
Description
+
Adds two or more operands
-
Subtract second operand from the first
*
Multiply first and second operand
/
Performs integer division and returns quotient
%
Performs modular division and returns remainder
**
Performs exponentiation
++
Increment variable value by 1
--
decrement variable value by 1
The bitwise operators supported by JavaScript are listed in the following table:
Let a=10 and b=11
Operator
Description
Example
&
Binary AND return bit 1 if both operands’ bits are 1, otherwise 0.
a&b=0000 1010
|
Binary OR return bit o if both operands bits are 0, otherwise 1.
a|b=0000 1011
^
Binary eXclusive OR (XOR) returns bit 0 if there are odd numbers of 1’s. Otherwise 1.
a^b=0000 1110
~
Binary negation complements the bits. Converts bits 1 to 0 and vice versa.
~a=0000 0101
<<
Binary left shift operator shifts the number of bits specified onto the left.
a<<2=0010 1000
>>
Binary right shift operator shifts the number of bits specified onto the right.
a>>2=0000 0010
Shorthand assignment operators: Shorthand assignment operators are a compact way of writing assignment statements in an expression.
The general form of a shorthand assignment operator is,
Operand operator=expression
The following table shows the use of assignment operators:
Operator
Assignment statements

Comparison operators: Let us consider, a=10 and b=20 then:

Description

Compares the values of two operands equal or not
>
Checks if the value of the operand on the left is greater than the right operand.
<
Checks if the value of the operand on the left is less than the right operand.
>=
Checks if the value of the operand on the left is greater than or equal to the right operand.
<=
Checks if the value of the operand on the left is less than or equal to the right operand.
===
Equal value and equal type
!==
Not equal value or not equal type
?
Ternary operator

Description
&&

||
Logical OR operator checks if both the operands are zero then condition becomes false, otherwise true.
!
Logical NOT operator reverses the state of the operand. If the operand is true then it makes it false and vice versa
Logical AND: the result of the logical AND is true, when both the operands are true; otherwise the result is false.

CHAPTER 13
DATA TYPES
Introduction: Data type is a type of data that the variable can hold. The computer doesn’t understand the values without data types. JavaScript can hold numbers, strings, objects and more data types.
The following example generates an error without the data types.
let a=10+”Hanumanth”;
in the example above, JavaScript will treat as,
let a=”10”+”Hanumanth”;
when adding a number and a string, JavaScript will treat the number as a string.
JavaScript evaluates expressions from left to right. Different sequences can produce different results as shown in the following example.
let a=10+5+”Hanumanth”;
returns, 15Hanumanth
here, JavaScript treats 10 and 5 as numbers, until it reaches “Hanumanth”
similarly, when we write
let a=”Hanumanth”+10+5
returns, Hanumanth105
Here, JavaScript encounters a string operand hence all operands are treated as strings.
JavaScript data types are Dynamic: Dynamic data types means; the same variable can hold different types of data as shown in the following example.
let a;​// a is undefined
a=6​// a has a number
a=”Hanumanth”​// a is a string
JavaScript Strings: A string is a sequence of characters enclosed within a pair of double quotation marks or a pair of single quotation marks as shown in the following example.
let FirstName=”Hanumanth”;​// Hanumanth written in double quotes
let LastName=’Ladwa’;​// Ladwa written in single quotes
if we want to display quotes in the output then we can use them as shown in the following example.
let Animal=”That’s an elephant”;​// single quote
let Fastest=”The fastest of animals is ‘leopard’ ”;​// single quotes
let Slowest=’The slowest of animals is “Tortoise” ‘;​//double quotes
example:
<html>
<head><title>Strings Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let Animal="That's an elephant";​// single quote
let Fastest="The fastest of animals is 'leopard' ";​// single quotes
let Slowest='The slowest of animals is "Tortoise" ';​//double quotes
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
Animal + "<br>" +
Fastest + "<br>" +
Slowest;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Numbers: JavaScript numbers can be written with or without decimals.
Example: let a=23.0;
let b=23;
the biggest and the smallest numbers can be written in scientific exponential notation.
Example: let x=12e3​// this is equivalent to 12000
let y=12e-3​// this is equivalent to 0.012
Boolean values: Boolean values return either TRUE or FALSE value.
Example:
let a=10;
let b=20;
let c=10;
(a==c)​​// This returns TRUE
(a==b)​// This returns FALSE
Boolean values are normally used with conditional (or selection) statements.
Arrays: Arrays are a collection of Homogeneous data items under the same name. each array item can be accessed with the help of its index number. Index number starts from 0 to N-1.
Example:
const names=[“Hanumanth”,”Priyanka”,”Sneha”,”Swaroop”,”Raj”,”Rakshita”];
Objects: JavaScript objects are written with curly braces and the object properties are written as name:value separated by commas.
Let’s have a look at the following example,
<html>
<head><title>Objects Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Objects Demo</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const person = {
firstName : "Hanumanth",
lastName  : "Ladwa",
Height     : 5.9,
complexion  : "Wheat"
};
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
person.firstName+" "+person.lastName+ " is " + person.Height + " tall and has "+person.complexion+" complexion ";
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
The typeof operator: we can use typeof operator to find the type of a variable. This operator returns the type of a variable or an expression.
<html>
<head><title>Typeof Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript typeof Demo</h2>

<script>
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
typeof 5 + "<br>" +
typeof 12e3 + "<br>" +
typeof "John Doe";
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Functions: A function is a collection of a finite set of instructions that performs a specific task. The function gets invoked when it is called by its name.
Advantages of using functions:
-          If there is a set of statements to be repeated several times in the program, these statements can be replaced as a function and called (or invoked) whenever and wherever required.
-          If a task is needed in more than one program or to a group of programmers, a function can be developed and made available to other programs or other programmers.
-          Programs can be modularized into functions. Each function can be developed independently, debugged and tested.
-          If a program is divided into sub-programs, a team of programmers can be formed to solve each subproblem, rather than being solved by a single person.
-          If functions are developed in a program, the number of lines of code decreases.
Syntax:
JavaScript function is defined with the function keyword, followed by function name and parameters in the parentheses. The code to be executed is written in curly braces as shown below.
function function_name(parameter 1, parameter 2, …)
{
// Code to be executed is written here
}
JavaScript functions are similar to procedure or a subroutine in other programming languages.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Function Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Functions</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
function add(a, b) {
return a + b;
}
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = add(4, 2);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
The code inside the curly braces will get executed when it is called. This process of executing code is called function invocation. The code gets executed in one of the following three events.
-          When an event occurs such as button click
-          When it is invoked from JavaScript code and
-          Automatically i.e., self-invoked.
When all the instructions written in a JavaScript function executes and encounters a return value, the code stops executing and returns the value back to the calling function. The following program demonstrates how to call a function and how the function code returns a value back to the caller.
<html>
<head><title>Function Call Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
var a = add(4, 2);
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = a;
function add(a, b) {
return a + b;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Instead of using variable a in the above program we can also use function call in place of a variable as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Function Call without using variable Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = add(4, 2);
function add(a, b) {
return a + b;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Local variables: The variables declared within the function definition are called local variables and they are accessible within the curly braces. It has its scope restricted to the code only, not outside it.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Local Variable Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Functions</h2>
<p>Outside myNameFunction() MyName is undefined.</p>

<p id="sample2"></p>
<script>
myNameFunction();
function myNameFunction() {
let MyName = "Hanumanth Ladwa";

typeof MyName + " " + MyName;
}
document.getElementById("sample2").innerHTML =
typeof MyName;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Real life objects, properties and methods: In real life, a man is an object.
A man has properties like weight, height, complexion etc and the methods like walk(), talk(), watch() etc.
Properties:
Man.name=Hanumanth
Man.weight=73 kgs
Man.height=5’.9”
Man.complexion=wheat etc.
Methods:
Man.walk()
Man.talk()
Man.watch() etc.
All men have the same properties but the property values differ from person to person. All men have the same methods, but the methods are perfumed at different times.
JavaScript objects: we have studied in the previous sections that the variables can hold values and it holds one value at a time, JavaScript objects are also variables but they hold many values, for example:
(Hanumanth, 73, 5.9, wheat) is assigned to a variable named Man.
Object properties can be accessed in two ways:
objectName.propertyName
OR
objectName[“propertyName”]
The following example demonstrates the use of multiple values to a JavaScript object.
<html>
<head><title>JavaScript Objects Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Objects Demo</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const man = {name:"Hanumanth", weight:"73kgs", height:"5.9", complexion:"wheat"};
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = "A man "+man.name+" has height " + man.height+" feet";
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Observe that the values of the properties are written as name:value pairs and it is a programming practice to declare objects with the const keyword.
Let’s have an example that demonstrates how to access the properties using the second method.
<html>
<head><title>JavaScript Objects Using second method Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Objects Demo</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const man = {name:"Hanumanth", weight:"73kgs", height:"5.9", complexion:"wheat"};

</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Objects also have methods. Methods are actions or operations that can be performed on objects.
Accessing object methods:
Syntax:
objectName.methodName()
The following example demonstrates accessing object methods.
<html>
<head><title>JavaScript Objects Method Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Methods Demo</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const man = {name:"Hanumanth", weight:"73kgs", height:"5.9", complexion:"wheat", details:function(){return this.name+" "+this.height;}};
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = man.details();
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
JavaScript Events: When JavaScript is used in HTML pages, JavaScript can react to these events.
HTML events: An HTML event can be something the browser does, or something a user does. Some examples of HTML events are given below.
-          An HTML web page has finished loading
-          An HTML input field was changed
-          An HTML button was clicked
JavaScript executes code when events are detected. HTML allows event handler attributes, with JavaScript code, to be added to HTML elements as shown below.
<element event=’some JavaScript’>
OR
<element event=”some JavaScript”>
Let’s have a look at the following example that deals with onclick attributes added to a <button> element.
<html>
<head><title>Onclick Button Demo</title></head>
<body>
<button onclick="document.getElementById('sample').innerHTML=Date()">Check Date</button>
<p id="sample"></p>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon click, we get the following output
Here, JavaScript code changes the content of the element with id=”sample”. In the example below, the code changes the content of its own element using this.innerHTML.
<html>
<head><title>Onclick Button this.innerHTML Demo</title></head>
<body>
<button onclick="this.innerHTML=Date()">Check Date</button>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon click, we get the following result.
We can also write the JavaScript code to display the date using the function as shown below.
<html>
<head><title>Display Date using function</title><head>
<body>
<button onclick="dateToday()">Check Date</button>
<script>
function dateToday() {
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = Date();
}
</script>
<p id="sample"></p>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon click, we get the following result.
Common HTML events are listed in the table below.
Event
Description
Onchange
An HTML element has been changed.
Onclick
The user clicks an HTML element.
Onmouseover
The user moves the mouse over an HTML element.
Onmouseout
The user moves the mouse away from an HTML element
Onkeydown
The user pushes a keyboard key
Onload
The browser has finished loading the page.
Let’s have some programs on these events

}
</script>
</body>
</html>

Number methods and properties: primitive values such as 123 or 3.142 etc cannot have properties and methods because they are not objects. In JavaScript, methods and properties are also available to primitive values because it treats primitive values as objects.
The toString method: This method returns a number as a string and it is implemented in the following example.

(450 + 6).toString();
</script>

</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: A parameter defines the number of characters behind the decimal point. And the parameter is optional. If you don’t specify it will not round the number.

CHAPTER 14
STRINGS
Introduction: A string is a sequence of characters enclosed within a pair of double or single quotation marks. Characters in the quotes may be zero or more.
Example: let name=”Hanumanth Ladwa”;
OR
let name=’Hanumanth Ladwa’;
string length can be determined by built-in length property.
Example: let name=”Hanumanth Ladwa”;
name.length;​// This will return 15
Escape character: These are the characters constants begin with backslash (\). In order to print the single or double quotes or backslash itself, we use the following escape sequence character constants.
\’​This prints a single quotation mark.
\”​This prints a double quotation mark.
\\​This prints a backslash itself.
Let’s have a look at the following example that demonstrates all the above three escape sequences.
<html>
<head><title>Use of escape sequences Demo</title><head>
<body>
<p id="sample1"></p>
<p id="sample2"></p>
<p id="sample3"></p>
<script>
let str1 = "We are glad to announce \"Bonus\" for all employees.";
document.getElementById("sample1").innerHTML = str1;
let str2 = "We are glad to announce \'Bonus\' for all employees.";
document.getElementById("sample2").innerHTML = str2;
let str3 = "We are glad to announce \\Bonus\\ for all employees.";
document.getElementById("sample3").innerHTML = str3;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Some other escape sequences are written as shown in the table below.
Escape sequence
Description
\b
Backspace
\f
Form feed
\n
New line
\r
Carriage return
\t
Horizontal tab space
\v
Vertical tab space
Strings can be objects: Strings are primitive types as we have already seen before.
Example: let name=”Hanumanth Ladwa”;
But the following statement shows that the string can also be defined as objects with the keyword new operator.
let name=new String(“Hanumanth Ladwa”);
let’s look at the following example that demonstrate string and object
<html>
<head><title>String Object Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let a = "Hanumanth Ladwa";        // a is a string
let b = new String("Hanumanth Ladwa");  // b is an object
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
"a is a "+typeof a + " and b is an " + typeof b;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: Avoid creating strings as objects because execution speed reduces and this can produce some unexpected results.
When we use == operator, equal strings are equal:
<html>
<head><title>When == Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let a = "Hanumanth Ladwa";
let b = new String("Hanumanth Ladwa");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
(a==b);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
When using the === operator, equal values may not be equal, because it expects equality in both data type and value.

(a===b);
</script>
</body>
</html>

String Properties and Methods:
String length: The length property returns the total number of characters in the string. Code example is already seen in the previous section.
slice(): This method extracts a part of a string. This method takes 2 parameters: start position and the end position.
syntax:
slice(start,end);
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Slice Method Demo</title></head>
<body>

let flower = "Sunflower, Rose, Lilly";

</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
If a parameter value is negative then the position is counted from the end of the string.
Example:

let flower = "Sunflower, Rose, Lilly";

</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
If you omit the second parameter, the method will slice out the rest of the string as shown in the following example.

let flower = "Sunflower, Rose, Lilly";

</script>
</body>

let flower = "Sunflower, Rose, Lilly";

</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
The substring() method: This method is similar to the slice() method but there is a difference, the substring() method will never receive negative indexes.

substring(start, end);
Let’s have a look at the example shown below.

</body>

If you omit the second parameter, substring() will slice out the rest of the string.
The substr() method: This method is similar to slice but the difference is that the second parameter is the length of the extracted part.
Syntax:
substr(start, length);
Let’s look at the example given below.

</script>
</body>

If you omit the second parameter, substr() will slice out the rest of the string.
Let’s have a look at the example given below.

let flower = "Sunflower, Rose, Lilly";

</body>

If the first parameter is negative, the position counts from the end of the string as shown in the following example.

The replace() method: String content can be replaced by using the method called replace() as shown in the following example.

<body>

<p id="sample">Come Office</p>

function replaceFunction() {

document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
str.replace("Office","Home");
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on the Replace button, the following result will be displayed.
The replace() method is case sensitive. i.e., both uppercase and lowercase letters are distinct. To make the replace() method to be case insensitive, use a regular expression with an /i flag as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Replace() method case insensitive Demo</title></head>
<body>
<button onclick="replaceFunction()">Replace</button>
<p id="sample">Come Office</p>
<script>
function replaceFunction() {
let str = document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
str.replace(/Office/i,"Home");
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on the Replace button, the following result is displayed.
Regular expression /g flag replaces all the matches as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Replace() method global match Demo</title></head>
<body>
<button onclick="replaceFunction()">Replace</button>
<p id="sample">Come Office and Office</p>
<script>
function replaceFunction() {
let str = document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
str.replace(/Office/g,"Home");
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on the Replace button the following result is displayed.

String.search() method: This method searches for a specified value and returns the position of the match as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>The String.search() method demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str = "My Home is Sweet Home";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str.search("Home");
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Notice that both indexOf() and search() methods are giving the same result. Are they the same? No, there is a difference in them. The search() method cannot take a second start position argument whereas, indexOf() method cannot take regular expressions.
String.match() method: This method is used to search a string for a match against a regular expression, and returns the matches as an array object.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The String.match() method demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str = "My Home is Sweet Home";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str.match(/Home/g);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: if the regular expression does not include the g modifier to perform a global search, the match() method will return only the first match in the string.
In order to perform a global, case insensitive search for the specific string we use the following code.
<html>
<head><title>The String.match() case insensitive method demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str = "My Home is Sweet HOME";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str.match(/Home/gi);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
String.includes() method: This method returns true if a string contains a specified value.
Syntax:
string.includes(searchvalue, start);
where searchvalue is the string to search for and start is option and its default value is 0. Start represents the position to start the search. This method returns true if the string contains the value, otherwise false.
Examples below demonstrate the same.
<html>
<head><title>The String.includes() method demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str = "My Home is Sweet Home";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str.includes("Home");
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Let’s have an example that returns false value and it uses start value as shown below.
<html>
<head><title>The String.includes() method uses start value demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str = "My Home is Sweet Home";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str.includes("My",3);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
String.startsWith() method: This method returns true if a string begins with a specified value, otherwise false.
Syntax:
string.startsWith(searchvalue, start);
where searchvalue is the string to search for and start is option and its default value is 0. Start represents the position to start the search. This method returns true if the string contains the value, otherwise false.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The String.startsWith() method demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str = "My Home is Sweet Home";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str.startsWith("Home");
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
String.endsWith() method: This method returns true if a string ends with a specified value, otherwise false.
Syntax:
string.endswith(searchvalue, length);
where, searchvalue is the value to search for.
Length is optional and it represents the length to search.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The String.endsWith() method demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str = "My Home is Sweet Home";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str.endsWith("Home");
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:

Interpolation: Template literals provide an easy way to interpolate variables and expressions into strings.
Syntax:
${…}
Variable substitutions: Template literals allow variables in strings as shown in the example below.
<html>
<head><title>Variable substitution Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let str1 = "to the world of ";
let str2 = "JavaScript";
let str = `Welcome ${str1}${str2}!`;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Automatic replacing of variables with real values is called string interpolation.
Expression substitution: Template literals also allow expression in strings as shown in the example below.
<html>
<head><title>Expression substitution Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let principal = 100;
let time = 2;
let rate = 6;
let si=`Simple Interest: ${((principal*time*rate)/100).toFixed(2)}`;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = si;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Automatic replacing of expressions with real values is called string interpolation.

CHAPTER 15
ARRAYS
Introduction: Arrays are the collection of values of the same type under the single variable. You can access each value by referring to an index number. In JavaScript it is a common practice to declare arrays with the const keyword.
Syntax:
const array_name=[item1, item2, …];
<html>
<head><title>Arrays Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const family_members = ["Priyanka", "Sneha", "Sonia", "Swaroop"];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = family_members;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Using a new keyword: with the help of a new operator, we create an array dynamically as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Array new Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const family_members = new Array("Priyanka", "Sneha", "Sonia", "Swaroop");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = family_members;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Each element of the array can be accessed with the help of its index number.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Array Accessing element Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const family_members = ["Priyanka", "Sneha", "Sonia", "Swaroop"];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = family_members[0];
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
We can also change the value of the specific subscript of the array as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Array Changing value Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const family_members = ["Priyanka", "Sneha", "Sonia", "Swaroop"];
family_members[1]="Raj";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = family_members;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Arrays are objects: objects use names to access its members as shown in this example.

</body>
</html>
Output:
Array elements can be objects: arrays can have different types because JavaScript arrays are kinds of objects.

Looping array elements: The best way to display all the array elements is through the for loop.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Accessing Array elements using for loop</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const family_members = ["Priyanka", "Sneha", "Sonia", "Swaroop"];
let l = family_members.length;
let str = "<ul>";
for (let i = 0; i < l; i++)
{
str += "<li>" + family_members[i] + "</li>";
}
str += "</ul>";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Instead of a for loop we can also use Array.forEach() function as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Accessing Array elements using forEach() function</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const family_members = ["Priyanka", "Sneha", "Sonia", "Swaroop"];
let l = family_members.length;
let str = "<ul>";
family_members.forEach(arrayFunction);
str += "</ul>";
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str;
function arrayFunction(value){
str += "<li>" + value + "</li>";
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Adding array elements: we can add elements to an array using push() method as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Adding array element Demo</title></head>

const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = flowers;

flowers.push("sunflower");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = flowers;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on the Add Element button we get the following result.
New element can also be added to an array using the length property as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Adding array element using length property</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<button onclick="flowerFunction()">Add Element</button>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>

document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = flowers;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on the Add Element button, we get the following result.
Adding elements with high indexes can create undefined holes in an array as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Adding array elements with higher indexes</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
flowers[5] = "Sunflower";
let l = flowers.length;
let str = "";
for (i = 0; i < l; i++) {
str += flowers[i] + "<br>";
}
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Arrays and objects: In JavaScript, arrays use numbered indexes whereas, objects use named indexes. We should use objects when we want the element names to be strings(text). We should use arrays when we want the element names to be numbers. JavaScript has a built-in array constructor new Array(). But we can safely use [] instead.
Example: const a=new Array(10,20,30,40,50);
const a = [10,20,30,40,50];
These two different statements create a new array containing 5 numbers.
The new keyword can produce an unexpected result as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Unexpected result in array using new</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p>Avoid using new Array().</p>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
var a = new Array(10);
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = a;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
In order to identify the variable as an array we use Array.isArray() as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Array.isArray() method Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Arrays</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = Array.isArray(flowers);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:

</body>

Popping and pushing items in an array: Inserting an element into the array is called pushing and deletion of an element from the array is called popping. Inserting an element in an array is done with the push() method and removal of element from an array is done with the pop() method.
Example:
<html>

const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];

flowers.push("Sunflower");
document.getElementById("sample2").innerHTML = flowers;
document.getElementById("sample3").innerHTML = flowers;
flowers.pop();
document.getElementById("sample4").innerHTML = flowers;
</script>
</body>

Numeric sort: by default, sort() method is used to arrange values as strings “20” is bigger than “100”, because “2” is bigger than “1”. This produces an incorrect result when sorting numbers. We can fix this by providing a compare function as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>The numeric sort demo</title></head>
<body>
<button onclick="numericFunction()">Sort Ascending</button>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const a = [40, 10, 20, 50, 30, 60];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = a;
function numericFunction() {
a.sort(function(a, b){return a - b});
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = a;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on the Sort Ascending button we get the result as shown below.
Finding the highest or lowest array value: As there are no built-in functions for finding the max or min value in an array, we can use the index to obtain the highest and lowest values as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>The Minimum and Maximum demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<button onclick="minFunction()">Min</button>
<p id="sample1"></p>
<button onclick="maxFunction()">Max</button>
<p id="sample2"></p>
<script>
const a = [40, 10, 20, 50, 30, 60];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = a;
function minFunction() {
a.sort(function(a, b){return a - b});
document.getElementById("sample1").innerHTML = a[0];
}
function maxFunction() {
a.sort(function(a, b){return b - a});
document.getElementById("sample2").innerHTML = a[0];
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on Min and Max buttons we get the following result.
Note: sorting a whole array is a very inefficient method if you only want to find the highest or lowest value.
Using Math.min() and Math.max() on an array: Math.min.apply and Math.max.apply is used to find the minimum and maximum number in an array as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>The Math.min() and Math.max() demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<button onclick="minFunction()">Min</button>
<p id="sample1"></p>
<button onclick="maxFunction()">Max</button>
<p id="sample2"></p>
<script>
const a = [40, 10, 20, 50, 30, 60];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = a;
function minFunction() {
document.getElementById("sample1").innerHTML = Math.min.apply(null,a);
}
function maxFunction() {
document.getElementById("sample2").innerHTML = Math.max.apply(null,a);
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Upon clicking on the Min and Max button we get the following result.
My Min/Max JavaScript Methods:
These functions loop through an array comparing each value with the lowest and highest found as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>The min() and max() methods demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<p>The highest number is <span id="sample1"></span>.</p>
<p>The lowest number is <span id="sample2"></span>.</p>
<script>
const a = [40, 10, 20, 50, 30, 60];
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = a;
document.getElementById("sample1").innerHTML = myArrayMax(a);
document.getElementById("sample2").innerHTML = myArrayMin(a);
function myArrayMax(arr) {
let len = arr.length;
let max = -Infinity;
while (len--) {
if (arr[len] > max) {
max = arr[len];
}
}
return max;
}
function myArrayMin(arr) {
let len = arr.length;
let min = Infinity;
while (len--) {
if (arr[len] < min) {
min = arr[len];
}
}
return min;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Sorting object arrays: Even if objects have properties of different data types, the sort() method can be used to sort the array.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Sorting Array Objects</title></head>
<body>
<button onclick="arrayFunction()">Sort It!</button>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const family_members = [
{type:"Sneha", year_of_birth:2008},
{type:"Priyanka", year_of_birth:2007},
{type:"Sonia", year_of_birth:2011}
];
displayYear();
function arrayFunction() {
family_members.sort(function(a, b){return a.year_of_birth - b.year_of_birth});
displayYear();
}
function displayYear() {
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
family_members[0].type + " " + family_members[0].year_of_birth + "<br>" +
family_members[1].type + " " + family_members[1].year_of_birth + "<br>" +
family_members[2].type + " " + family_members[2].year_of_birth;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Array Iteration: Array iteration means accessing each element of the array at least once to perform some operation called traversing. There are several methods on array iteration as listed below.
Array.forEach(): This method calls a function once for each array element.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The forEach() method Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const a = [30, 40, 20, 10, 50];
let str = "";
a.forEach(arrayFunction);
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str;
function arrayFunction(value, index, array) {
str += value + "<br>";
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note that the function takes three arguments, the item value, the item index and the array itself.
Array.map(): This method creates a new array by performing a function on each array element. It does not execute the function for array elements without values. It does not change the original array.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The Array.map() method Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const a = [30, 40, 20, 10, 50];
const b = a.map(arrayFunction);
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = b;
function arrayFunction(value, index, array) {
return value * 5;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:

</body>

Output:
Array.indexOf() method: This method searches an array for an element value and returns its position.
Syntax:
array.indexOf(item, start);
here, item – the item to search for.
start – where to start the search. The negative values will start at the given position counting from the end, and search to the end.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The Array.indexOf() method Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly", "Lotus", "Sunflower"];
let p = flowers.indexOf("Lilly")+1;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = "The element \"Lilly\" is found at position "+p;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: Array.indexOf() returns -1 if the item is not found. If the element is present more than once, it returns the position of the first occurrence.
Array.lastIndexOf() method: This function returns the position of the last occurrence of the specified element.
Syntax:
array.lastIndexOf(item, start);
here, item – the item to search for.
start – where to start the search. The negative values will start at the given position counting from the end, and search to the beginning.
<html>
<head><title>The Array.lastIndexOf() method Demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly", "Lotus", "Sunflower"];
let p = flowers.lastIndexOf("Lilly")+1;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = "The element \"Lilly\" is found at position "+p;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Array.includes() method: This method is used to check if an element is present in an array.
Syntax:
Array.includes(search-item);
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The Array.includes() method Demo</title></head>

Array const: As we know that, it is a common practice to declare arrays using const. An array declared with const cannot be reassigned. If we try to do so, then the error is encountered as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Cannot Reassign a constant array</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
try {
const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
flowers = ["Sunflower", "Lotus", "Kewda"];
}
catch (err) {
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = err;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Array elements can be reassigned as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>Can Reassign a constant array</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
flowers[0]="Sunflower";
flowers.push("Kewda");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = flowers;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: const variables must be assigned a value when they are declared. Otherwise, the syntax error is encountered.
Const block scope: An array declared with const has block scope.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Declaring array with block scope</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
{
const flowers = ["Sunflower", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
}
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = flowers[0];
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
An array declared with var does not have block scope.
<html>
<head><title>Declaring array without block scope</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
var flowers = ["Rose", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
{
var flowers = ["Sunflower", "Jasmin", "Lilly"];
}
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = flowers[0];
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Redeclaring arrays: redeclaring an array declared with var is allowed anywhere in a program.
Example:
var flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];
var flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];
flowers=[“Sunflower”, Kewda];
redeclaring an array to const, in the same scope, or in the same block, is not allowed.
Example:
var flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​//allowed
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
{
var flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​//allowed
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
}
Redeclaring an existing const array, in the same scope, or in the same block, is not allowed.
Example:
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// allowed
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
var flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
{
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// allowed
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
var flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
}
Redeclaring an array with const, in another scope, or in another block, is allowed.
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// allowed
{
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// not allowed
}
{
const flowers=[“Rose”, “Jasmin”, “Lilly”];​// allowed
}

CHAPTER 16
DATES
Introduction: JavaScript uses the browser’s time zone by default and displays a date. The date object is created with the help of the new Date() constructor.
There are four ways to create a new date object:
-          new Date()
-          new Date(year, month, day, hours, minutes, seconds, milliseconds)
-          new Date(milliseconds)
-          new Date(date string)
new Date(): It is used to create a date object with the current date and time as shown in the following example:
<html>
<head><title>The Date() constructor demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date();
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
new Date(year, month, day, hours, minutes, seconds, milliseconds): This creates a new date object with a specified date and time. 7 numbers specify year, month, day, hour, minute, second, and millisecond.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The Date() constructor with 7 numbers demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date(2021, 08, 23, 20, 15, 0, 0);
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: JavaScript counts months from 0 to 11: January = 0 and December = 11. In the above example, you can observe 08 represents September month.
Specifying a month higher than 11, will not result in an error but add the overflow to the next year.
Specifying a day higher than max, will not result in an error in an error but add the overflow to the next month.
Six numbers specify year, month, day, hour, minute, second.
Five numbers specify year, month, day, hour, minute.
Four numbers specify year, month, day, hour.
Three numbers specify year, month, day.
Two numbers specify year, month.
Note: we cannot omit a month. If we supply only one parameter it will be treated as milliseconds.
Previous century: one and two digit years will be interpreted as 19xx.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The new century demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date(99, 08, 23);
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
new Date(dateString): It creates a new date object from a date string as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>The date string demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date("September 23, 2021 20:32:00");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
new Date(milliseconds): It creates a new date object as zero time plus milliseconds. JavaScript stores dates as the number of milliseconds since January 01, 1970, 00:00:00 UTC (Universal Time Coordinated).
Example:
<html>
<head><title>The new Date(milliseconds) demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date(0);
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Date Methods: There are numerous methods to set day, month, year and so on.
The toDateString() method: This method is used to convert a date to a more readable format as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>The toDateString() demo</title></head>
<body>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date();
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d.toDateString();
</script>
</body>
</html>

JavaScript Date Input: There are three types of date formats:
-          ISO Date
-          Short Date
-          Long Date
JavaScript Date Output: JavaScript output dates in full text string format as shown below.
Fri Sept 24 2021 20:40:00 GMT+0530 (India Standard Time)
ISO Dates: ISO 8601 is the international standard for the representation of dates and times.
Syntax: (YYYY-MM-DD)
Example:
<html>
<head><title>ISO Date Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript ISO Dates</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date("2021-09-24");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
ISO dates (year and month): Without day also, we can use ISO dates as (YYYY-MM).
ISO dates (only year): Without month and day, we can use ISO dates as (YYYY).
ISO dates (Date-Time): ISO dates with time can be written as (YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ).
Example:
<html>
<head><title>ISO Date with time Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript ISO Dates</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date("2021-09-24T20:52:00Z");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Date and time are separated with a capital T and UTC time is defined with a capital Z.
UTC (Universal Time Coordinated) is the same as GMT (Greenwich Mean Time).
Omitting T or Z in a date-time string can give different results in different browsers.
Time zones: When we do not specify the time zone, by default JavaScript will use the browser’s time zone. If a date/time is created in GMT, the date/time will be converted to CDT (Central US Daylight Time) if a user browsers from the central US.
Short Dates: Short dates are written in “MM/DD/YYYY” format.
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Short Date Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript ISO Dates</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
const d = new Date("09/24/2021");
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = d;
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: Some browsers may produce an error if months or days have no leading zeros. Some browsers will return NaN.
Long dates: Long dates are normally written in a “MMM DD YYYY” format as shown in the following example.

let str;

const someday = new Date();
someday.setFullYear(2021, 8, 24);
if (someday > today) {
str = "Today is before September 24, 2021.";
} else {
str = "Today is after September 24, 2021.";
}
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = str;
</script>
</body>

CHAPTER 17
MATHEMATICAL AND COMPARISON OPERATIONS
Introduction: In JavaScript Math objects are used to perform mathematical operations on numbers.
Math property syntax:
Math.property
Math properties or constants provide 8 mathematical constants as shown in the following example.
Math.E        // returns Euler's number
Math.PI       // returns PI
Math.SQRT2    // returns the square root of 2
Math.SQRT1_2  // returns the square root of 1/2
Math.LN2      // returns the natural logarithm of 2
Math.LN10     // returns the natural logarithm of 10
Math.LOG2E    // returns base 2 logarithm of E
Math.LOG10E   // returns base 10 logarithm of E
<html>
<head><title>Math Constants Demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Math Constants</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>

document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
"<p><b>Math.E:</b> " + Math.E + "</p>" +
"<p><b>Math.PI:</b> " + Math.PI + "</p>" +
"<p><b>Math.SQRT2:</b> " + Math.SQRT2 + "</p>" +
"<p><b>Math.SQRT1_2:</b> " + Math.SQRT1_2 + "</p>" +
"<p><b>Math.LN2:</b> " + Math.LN2 + "</p>" +
"<p><b>Math.LN10:</b> " + Math.LN10 + "</p>" +
"<p><b>Math.LOG2E:</b> " + Math.LOG2E + "</p>" +
"<p><b>Math.Log10E:</b> " + Math.LOG10E + "</p>";
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Math methods: There are wide varieties of mathematical methods in JavaScript. Some of them are discussed in the following section.
Syntax:
Math.method(number);
Number to integer methods: There are some methods in JavaScript that are used to round a number to an integer. The following are the list of methods that round off a number to an integer.
Math.round(x)
Returns x rounded to its nearest integer
Math.ceil(x)
Returns x rounded up to its nearest integer
Math.floor(x)
Returns x rounded down to its nearest integer
Math.trunc(x)
Returns the integer part of x 
Math.round() method: This method returns the nearest integer.
Example
Math.round(5.8);    // returns 6
Math.round(4.9);    // returns 5
Math.round(5.4);    // returns 5
Math.round(5.2);    // returns 5
Math.round(-3.1);    // returns -3
Math.ceil() method: This method returns the value of the argument rounded up to its nearest integer.
Example:
Math.ceil(5.8);    // returns 6
Math.ceil(4.9);    // returns 5
Math.ceil(5.4);    // returns 6
Math.ceil(5.2);    // returns 6
Math.ceil(-3.1);    // returns -3
Math.floor() method: This method is used to return the value of an argument rounded down to its nearest integer.
Example:
Math.floor(5.8);    // returns 5
Math.floor(4.9);    // returns 4
Math.floor(5.4);    // returns 5
Math.floor(5.2);    // returns 5
Math.floor(-3.1);    // returns -4
Math.trunc() method: This method is used to return the integer part of the argument.
Example:
Math.floor(5.8);    // returns 5
Math.floor(4.9);    // returns 4
Math.floor(5.4);    // returns 5
Math.floor(5.2);    // returns 5
Math.floor(-3.1);    // returns -3
Math.sign() method: This method is used to return negative if an argument is negative, positive if an argument is positive and null if an argument is not a number.
Example:
Math.sign(-5.8);    // returns -1
Math. sign (4.9);    // returns 1
Math. sign (0);    // returns 0
Math. sign (A);    // returns NULL
Math.pow() method: This method returns the value of x to the power of y.
Example:
Math.pow(2,5);​//returns 32
Math.sqrt() method: This method returns the square root of an argument.
Example:
Math,sqrt(25);​​// returns 5
Math.abs() method: This method returns the positive value of an argument.
Example:
Math.abs(-5.1)​// returns 5.1
Math.sin() method: This method returns the sine value between -1 and 1 of the angle x given in radians.
Note: If you want to change radians to degree, use the formula given below.
Angle of radians=Angle in  degrees x PI/180.
Example:
Math.sin(90*Math.PI/180);​// returns 1
Math.cos() method: This method returns the cosine value between -1 and 1 of the angle x given in radians.
Note: If you want to change radians to degree, use the formula given below.
Angle of radians=Angle in  degrees x PI/180.
Example:
Math.cos(0*Math.PI/180);​// returns 1
Math.min() method: This method is used to find the lowest value in a list of arguments.
Example:
Math.min(12,10,3,-2,33,-5);​// returns -5
Math.max() method: This method is used to find the highest value in a list of arguments.
Example:
Math.max(12,10,3,-2,33,-5);​// returns 33
Math.random() method: This method returns a random number between 0 (inclusive) and 1 (exclusive).
Math.random();​// returns a random number
Math.log() method: This method returns the natural logarithm of an argument.
Example:
Math.log(1)​​// returns 0
Math.log2() method: This method returns the base 2 logarithm of an argument.
Example:
Math.log2(8);​// returns 3
Math.log10() method: This method returns the base 10 logarithm of an argument.
Example:
Math.log10(1000);​​// returns 3
All the above mathematical methods are covered in the following example.

The Math.random() method: We have already covered the Math.random() method in the previous section. As we have come across that the random() method generates numbers between 0 (inclusive), and 1 (exclusive). It always returns a number less than 1.
Random integers: Math.random() used with Math.floor() can be used to return random integers as shown in the following example.
// Returns a random integer from 0 to 9:
Math.floor(Math.random() * 10);
<html>
<head><title>Random integer demo</title></head>
<body>

</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
// Returns a random integer from 0 to 10:
Math.floor(Math.random() * 11);
// Returns a random integer from 0 to 99:
Math.floor(Math.random() * 100);
Note: We can multiply any number with the random() method. The numbers will be generated randomly within that range.
A proper random function: A proper random function always returns a random number between min (included) and max (excluded) as shown in the following example.
<html>
<head><title>A proper random method demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Math.random()</h2>
<button onclick="document.getElementById('sample').innerHTML = getRndInteger(0,10)">Get Random Number!</button>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
function getRndInteger(min, max) {
  return Math.floor(Math.random() * (max - min)) + min;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
The code below returns a random number between min and max both included.
<html>
<head><title>A proper random method included both min and max demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>A proper random method included both min and max demo</h2>
<button onclick="document.getElementById('sample').innerHTML = getRndInteger(0,10)">Get Random Number!</button>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
function getRndInteger(min, max) {
  return Math.floor(Math.random() * (max - min+1)) + min;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Boolean values: JavaScript supports Boolean values that result in either TRUE or FALSE. In some situations, we need a data type that returns one of the two values, like yes/no, on/off, TRUE/FALSE, high voltage/low voltage, bit 1/ bit 0. In order to represent these types of data we use Boolean data type.
The Boolean() function: This function is used to find out if an expression or a variable is true. Let’s have a look at the example below that returns true value.
Boolean(6>5)​// returns true
<html>
<head><title>The Boolean function demo</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Booleans</h2>
<h4>Display the value of Boolean(6>5)<h4>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = Boolean(6 > 5);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Everything with a value greater than and less than zero is true. Value zero or without a value is false.
Example: demonstrate the value greater than and less than zero is true
<html>
<head><title>Booleans returns true</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Booleans</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
"10 is " + Boolean(10) + "<br>" +
"1.02 is " + Boolean(1.02) + "<br>" +
"-5 is " + Boolean(-5) + "<br>" +
"Any (not empty) string is " + Boolean("Hi") + "<br>" +
"Even the string 'false' is " + Boolean('false') + "<br>" +
"Any expression (except zero) is " + Boolean(2 + 3 + 1.02);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Example: demonstrate the value zero or without a value is false.
<html>
<head><title>Booleans returns false</title></head>
<body>
<h2>JavaScript Booleans</h2>
<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
let a,b=null,c=false;
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML =
"0 is " + Boolean(0) + "<br>" +
"-0 is " + Boolean(-0) + "<br>" +
"empty string is " + Boolean("") + "<br>" +
"undefined is " + Boolean(a) + "<br>" +
"Null is false is " + Boolean(b) + "<br>" +
"Boolean value false is " + Boolean(false) + "<br>" +
"The Boolean value NaN is "+Boolean(5/"hi");
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Booleans can be objects: Booleans are primitive values created from literals.
Example
let a=false;
this same statement can also be written as,
let b=new Boolean(false);
Now, we have created an object with the keyword new.
Note: Boolean objects slow down execution speed. The new keyword can produce an unexpected result.
Example:

let b = new Boolean(false);  // b is an object
document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = (a==b);
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
When using the === operator, equal Booleans are not equal, because this operator expects equality in both type and value as shown in the following example.

</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Note: Objects cannot be compared. The difference between (a==b) and (a===b) and comparing two objects will always return false values.
Comparison operators: comparison and logical operators return either true or false value. Comparison operators are used logical statements or selection statements to determine equality or difference between variables or values.
Let us consider, a=6. The following table shows all comparison operators.
Operator
Description
==
Equal to

Not equal to

<

Less than or equal to
Logical operators: Logical operators are used to return Boolean values i.e., either true or false values. These logical operators can be used with comparison operators.
Let us consider, a=2 and b=3, the logical operators are explained in the table below.
Operator
Description
&&

Logical OR operator

Logical NOT operator
Conditional or ternary operator: The operators that operate on three or more operands are called conditional operators.
Syntax:
variablename=(condition) ? value1 : value2;
Example:
<html>
<head><title>Ternary operator demo</title></head>
<body>

<p id="sample"></p>
<script>
function ternaryFunction() {
  let n = document.getElementById("even").value;
  let even = (n%2==0) ? "Even Number":"Odd Number";
  document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = even;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
Output:
Clicking on Check it! Button we get the following result.
We can also change the value in the text box and click on Check it! Button and see the result shown below.
If the value of the variable is even then it prints an even number otherwise, an odd number.
Comparing different types: When comparing a string with a number, JavaScript will convert the string to a number. An empty string converts to zero. A non-numeric string converts to NaN which is always false.

Result

False

<html>

function ternaryFunction() {
  let even;
  let n = Number(document.getElementById("even").value);
  if(isNaN(n)){
  even="Input is not a number";
  }
else
  {
  even = (n%2==0) ? "Even Number":"Odd Number";
  }
  document.getElementById("sample").innerHTML = even;
}
</script>
</body>
</html>
